  - **`EyeShading`** gains `layer` (`EyeLayer`: `Eye`, the default, then `Shadow`, `Iris` and `Highlight(HighlightMotion)`, in drawing order) and `iris` (`IrisShape`: an ellipse in texture coordinates, center and half-extents, for the parallax's dome), and fixes what C5 left unread: `parallax` is the iris's depth below the cornea in meters (a few millimeters for an eye a few centimeters wide), and `mask_map` is the eye mask. `HighlightMotion` holds the drift (meters across the model's front) and its rate, and the sparkle (0 none, 1 twice as bright) and its rate; by default 0.3 mm at 0.3 Hz and 0.6 at 0.4 Hz.
  - **Validation:** the shadow, iris and highlight layers must blend (`AlphaMode::Blend`) and the eye itself must not, since it writes the eye depth mask; the depth, drift, sparkle and rates are non-negative and an iris's radii above 0.
  - **The block keeps its layout:** an eye's `surface.x` is the depth; `surface_color` is the iris ellipse on the eye and iris layers (zero for none) or a highlight's motion; the layer is `modes.w`'s third byte (`AnimeCharacterParams::EYE_LAYER_SHIFT`, 16), above the hair highlight style's second (`HIGHLIGHT_STYLE_SHIFT`, 8, where it was). **Decision: no version bump** (`Material::VERSION` stays W6's 7, `Mesh::VERSION` 10). Before C6 the shader read neither the eye's parallax nor its mask, so their meaning was free to fix; a document without the new fields packs the bytes it packed before, and no committed source changes its cooked block. `MaterialFlags::THROUGH_HAIR` now also marks blended eye layers, which draw through the bangs by their coverage; a blended face still never does.
  - **`character_maps::eye_mask`:** R the iris's depth below the cornea, 0 level with it (sclera, limbal ring) to 1 the full depth (the pupil), as WuWa's eye mask paints it. It also holds what the shader and its tests compute alike: the dome without a mask (`1 − |q|²` over the ellipse, so the limbal ring holds still and the pupil shifts most), the instances' phases (the golden ratio's step a slot), the drift and the sparkle.
- **As built (M1 A4, `sapphire_assets::{pack, AssetServer}`, `sapphire_platform::IoThread`):**
  - **Packs** (`.spack`, version 1): a 256-byte header (magic, version, counts, the table of contents' blake3, a hash of the header's first 128 bytes, and a signature block of scheme, the signing key's ID and 64 signature bytes, zero while unsigned); a table of contents of 80-byte entries sorted by ID (kind, codec, size, the asset's blake3, its blocks and its dependencies), 48-byte block records (offset, stored length, codec, the stored bytes' blake3) and the dependency IDs; then block data, each block 16-byte aligned. Each asset is exactly its cooked bytes, at most 1 GiB (three times the largest texture the engine cooks, so a crafted pack can't make a reader allocate more), cut into 64 KiB blocks compressed independently, so any block decodes alone; a block that doesn't shrink by 1/64 is stored raw. Mounting checks the header hash, the table's hash and every structural invariant (order, counts, ranges, layout, the file's length); decoding checks each block's hash before decompressing it, the asset's hash after, then validates the asset as its kind and compares its references with the listed dependencies. Signing comes with `sapphire_patch` (M4): a signature will cover the header hash, so the reserved block keeps the layout; readers reject any scheme today. Corrupt, truncated and hostile packs are errors, never panics: tests flip every byte of a small pack, and cover bad headers, tables, blocks, truncation and malformed compressed blocks.
  - **Decision: codecs.** zstd is the reference C library (`zstd` 0.13, libzstd 1.5.7, built with cc for every target, as the tools already built it) rather than the pure-Rust `ruzstd`. Measured on the M5, one core, 64 KB blocks of the cooked mannequin and of BC7 and ASTC textures: libzstd decodes 1.0–1.3 GB/s and ruzstd 0.9 0.27–0.35 GB/s, which on a Mobile-Low phone is the difference between a zone load bound by flash reads and one bound by decoding; ruzstd's encoder also stops at its fastest level (the mannequin at 0.57 of its size, against level 19's 0.37). Blocks are hash-checked before they are decoded, so corruption never reaches the C decoder; ruling out hostile packs is the signatures' job (M4), and until then they meet libzstd's own checks on malformed frames. lz4 is `lz4_flex` 0.14: pure Rust, no unsafe code, 4–5.5 GB/s. Which kind gets which codec is the packer's (§5.27).
  - **Load pipeline:** `mount` reads a pack's header and table, which become the runtime's only index from ID to data (rule R5). `load` returns a handle at once and requests the asset's dependencies first, which must be of a lower kind (meshes on materials, materials on textures), so no pack can make chains deep or circular; loading an asset that failed tries it again, with its failed dependencies; each asset is one positioned read of its blocks through the app's `PackSource` (the platform's IO thread, §5.1, by priority); the read's completion starts a decode job on the async compute pool (`sapphire_jobs::spawn_async`), whose result comes back over a channel that `update` drains. Cooked formats use relative pointers, so the decode job's validation is the whole fix-up: assets are read in place from then on. A decoded asset waits until its dependencies are ready, then joins the upload queue, and `upload` hands queued assets to the app's uploader as plain cooked data until the frame's budget is spent (16 MiB desktop, 4 MiB mobile; one asset always goes, however large). The renderer sees cooked data and its own handles, never the server (rule R4). Meshes and textures drop their decoded bytes once uploaded; materials keep theirs for CPU readers. A6's rigs (`.srig`), animation's CPU data, are a kind of their own that never goes to the GPU: ready once decoded, bytes kept. `reupload_all` serves a new renderer (device loss, a tier change), reading again what was dropped.
  - **Handles:** `Handle<T>` (`Mesh`, `Material`, `TextureAsset`, A6's `CharacterRig`) is one `Arc` pointer: clone and drop are an atomic each and allocate nothing, so dropping handles is allowed in the fixed step (rule R6); the server sees an asset's last user go by the count on its next update. `WeakHandle<T>` is a slotmap key (index and generation, 8 bytes, `Copy`) that resolves to nothing once its asset is unloaded, even after the slot is reused. Strong handles are checked against the server they came from.
  - **Residency:** per-kind budgets: meshes 768 MiB, textures 2.5 GiB, materials 32 MiB and rigs 64 MiB on desktop; 192 MiB, 600 MiB, 8 MiB and 16 MiB on mobile (the texture numbers are §3.4's zone pools, the others proposals). Unused assets stay cached until their kind is over budget, then go least recently used first, skipping pinned ones; assets in use are never unloaded, so a kind can stay over budget, which the stats show. `unload_unused` drops the cache on `LowMemory`. Unloads reach the app with the asset's slot, so it frees only that load's GPU copy: textures are freed; the renderer keeps mesh and material slots until it gains residency of its own.
  - **Material slots resolve:** a slot bound to a material asset loads its material and the material's textures first, and `Renderer::upload_slot_materials` takes their handles. A GPU test draws a packed sphere through a packed material sampling a packed texture.
  - **The sandbox** loads the mannequin by its ID (`8659a15c-…`) from `shared.spack` on every platform: from the content directory on desktop, the app bundle on iOS, and the APK's assets on Android, where Gradle's `noCompress` stores the pack as it is so block reads seek. Captures are byte-identical to path loading in all six golden scenes, and the per-frame allocations of `--bench` are unchanged (488.3). Mounting takes 0.1–0.6 ms and the mannequin is on the GPU 0.7–0.8 ms later (M5, debug build, under load from parallel builds).
  - **Stats (rule R9):** `AssetStats` (assets by state, unused and pinned, bytes resident per kind against budget, the last frame's uploads, reads in flight, loads, unloads and failures), in the debug overlay's stats panel.
  - **Tests:** pack round trips, random block access and the corruption cases above; strong and weak handles and generation reuse; residency's budget, LRU order and pinning; dependency order, kinds and failure propagation, and retrying failed assets; the per-frame upload budget; and the soak test: 300 rounds of loading and dropping characters under tight budgets (about 2,800 loads and unloads), after which no slot, byte or weak handle survives and live heap bytes are back exactly at their baseline (a counting allocator).
- **As built (M1 A8, `AssetServer::remount`, `sapphire_hot_reload`):**
  - **Decision: the desktop trigger watches the packs**, in the content root apps already read (`target/content/<cook>/`), not the loose cooked files (`target/content/loose/<cook>/`, which stay the tools'). A pack is written whole, to a temporary file renamed into place, after every asset of its cook, so an asset and its dependencies always come from one cook, where loose files land one by one (a material could be seen naming a texture not yet written). Remounting goes through the load pipeline itself, by asset ID through the table of contents (rule R5), where loose files would need an index from paths to IDs and kinds the runtime otherwise never has. And it is live link's path too: a phone has no cook directory, and A9 pushes packs. So `cargo xtask content` after an edit is what reaches the running app; a cook that changes nothing rewrites nothing.
  - **Remount:** `AssetServer::remount(name)` reads a mounted pack's header and table of contents again and checks them as `mount` does. A pack whose header is unchanged (it holds the table's hash) changes nothing; one that fails to read or check, or holds another mounted pack's asset, keeps the mounted version. Otherwise the new table becomes the index of the pack's assets, every loaded asset of the pack moves to its new entry (all of them before any load starts, so a load that reaches a dependency finds it where the new pack holds it), and each is compared with its entry by blake3 hash: an unchanged asset only moves to its new entry (and a read in flight is requested again, since the old file is about to close); a changed one in use or pinned loads its new version; a changed one nothing uses is unloaded (the cache's copy is stale; the next load reads the new one); one still loading starts again from the new pack, dependencies included; a failed one still in use is tried again; one the pack no longer holds keeps its version until nothing uses it (`AssetChange::Removed`), but can't be read again, so a new renderer can't get it unless its kind keeps its bytes. An asset moving to another mounted pack isn't followed yet (group packs, M2). The old file is then closed (`PackSource::close`, new, a no-op by default; the sandbox's closes the IO thread's file). `RemountReport` counts changed, removed and reloading assets.
  - **Swapping behind handles:** a ready asset loads its new version (`Slot::next`) while it stays ready and readable: the read goes through the same IO thread and decode job, its dependencies (from the new table) load first, and it joins the same upload queue and per-frame budget, after any dependency that is itself reloading, so a material binds its texture's new version. The uploader gets it as the asset's next **generation** for the same `AssetSlot` (`Upload::generation`): an app holding that slot at a lower generation replaces its copy in place, one holding it at the same generation (assets are sent again after `reupload_all`) keeps it. Once uploaded (or decoded, for rigs) it replaces the old version behind every handle (`get` reads it), dependencies only the old version had are released, `AssetServer::generation` goes up and `take_changes` reports `AssetChange::Reloaded`: CPU systems that derive data from an asset (animation from a rig, M2) compare generations or read the changes. A new version that fails (a corrupt block, an invalid asset, a dependency that won't load, an upload the app refuses) is dropped and reported (`ReloadFailed`); the asset keeps its version. New versions waiting on one that is dropped (failed, superseded by an edit undone, its asset gone) go on with the version it has.
  - **Dependents follow:** kinds that bind their dependencies' GPU objects (`AssetKind::binds_dependencies`: materials, whose group 3 holds their textures) are sent again from their kept bytes, as a generation of their own, when a dependency is replaced; a compile-time check keeps such kinds keeping their bytes. A mesh names its materials by the renderer's handles, which a material updated in place keeps, so it needs nothing. A new renderer taking over while a reload is in flight (`reupload_all`) gets the new version, as the next generation, and materials re-sent to it after their texture changed come as one too, so an app that keeps its old renderer rebinds them.
  - **Live link (A9)** drives the same path from a device: `MemoryPackSource::insert` replaces a pack (it now numbers each open file, closing frees it) and `remount` swaps in what changed. Pushing only the changed assets, as a pack mounted over the shared one, is A9's (`sapphire_assets` crate docs, later milestones).
- **As built (M1 A9, the devices' trigger):** live link (§5.29) pushes packs to a running dev build, sending only the assets that changed, and remounts them through the path above; the asset server is unchanged. **Decision:** no overlay mounts. A dev build's server reads through live link's own `PackSource` (`sapphire_livelink::LivePacks`), which serves a pushed pack whole, assembled on the device from the changed assets it received and the rest of the version it had (the app's own file, kept open while a version reads it), so the server keeps one mount per pack and sees the tool's pack byte for byte, every block still hash-checked before it is decoded. A pack the server refuses on remount is served no more. `MemoryPackSource` stays for tests and tools.
  - **Desktop trigger:** `sapphire_hot_reload`, a new `dev_only` crate. `notify` 8.2 (FSEvents, inotify, ReadDirectoryChangesW) watches directories, not recursively, behind a trailing-edge debounce on a thread of its own: a burst is reported once its files have been quiet for 100 ms, however long it lasted, each file once. `notify-debouncer-full` was tried first and dropped: it reports each event once that event is older than the timeout, found by a thread waking every quarter of it, so a burst longer than that splits into several reports, and idle it wakes 40 times a second; its rename stitching isn't needed, since a rename's event names both files. `HotReload` watches the content root's packs (a changed `<name>.spack`, written, renamed into place or removed, is a pack to remount; the temporary files packs are written to are ignored), the renderer's WGSL sources and documents, and `poll` (once a frame, one empty channel read when nothing changed, no allocation) reports what changed and reads the changed files. Where the engine's sources are comes from the crate's place in the repository at compile time (`sources::shader_dir`, `sources::default_day`, `sources::default_light_rigs`); tests check they are the files the engine compiles in.
  - **Dev builds only, desktop only:** the sandbox reaches the crate through its `dev` feature, and its `notify` feature (the backend, rule R8; on by default only for the crate's own tests) only through a dependency declared for non-mobile targets, so iOS and Android dev builds compile it without file watching (`HotReload::new` reports `Unsupported`) and link no `notify`; `cargo tree` for both targets finds none. `xtask check-layers` gains a `[dev_only_dependencies]` list (`egui`, `egui-wgpu`, `notify`): a runtime or app crate reaching one except through a dev-only crate fails the check, so a build without `dev` can't link the overlay or the watcher. `notify` 8.2.1 is CC0-1.0; it brings `notify-types`, `walkdir` and `same-file`, plus `objc2-core-services` (FSEvents) on macOS and `inotify` and `mio` on Linux, and shares the workspace's `windows-sys` 0.61 on Windows; `cargo deny` passes.
  - **Non-asset documents:** watched documents are reported with their text, for the app to parse and apply. The sandbox reloads two, both compiled into `sapphire_world` and watched in one directory: the default day (`default_day.ron`), whose timeline, if it parses and is complete, replaces time of day's base timeline (`TimeOfDay::set_timeline`), and the character light rig presets (`default_light_rigs.ron`, M1 C2), whose library, if it parses and is valid, replaces the scene's `LightRigLibrary` resource, characters keeping their preset IDs (§5.21's fallback for an ID past a shorter library's end). Either one that doesn't keeps the old. The quality-tier table and the sandbox's bindings wait (`sapphire_hot_reload` crate docs, later milestones).
  - **The sandbox:** on by default in `dev` builds (`--no-hot-reload` turns it off), in the windowed app and the offscreen modes alike, applied each frame before loading runs. Its uploader keeps each asset's slot and generation and replaces in place: a texture is uploaded anew (materials binding it are rebound when they come again), a material rewrites its slot (`Renderer::update_material`), a mesh replaces its streams under its handle (`Renderer::update_mesh`, §5.12), so the scene's handles stay valid. A renderer made later (a tier change, device loss) gets the shader sources an edit gave the last one. Errors are logged and listed in the overlay's stats panel (§5.20). A headless run (`--bench` with `--swatches`, release, dev) showed it end to end: the watcher started 80 ms after the content; a syntax error appended to `sky.wgsl` was refused as `sky.wgsl:32:12: expected identifier` and the sky kept drawing, a valid edit rebuilt the sky program's one pipeline, and restoring the file rebuilt it again; a valid edit to `default_day.ron` reloaded it and a broken one was refused (`Non-whitespace trailing characters`); editing a swatch material's base color and running `cargo xtask content` remounted `shared` (1 changed, 1 reloading) and swapped the material in as generation 1, and restoring it as generation 2. Run again on W2's sky (`--swatches`, 640×360): a syntax error in `sky.wgsl` was refused at its line, a comment rebuilt the sky's six pipelines in 4.7 ms, and `default_light_rigs.ron` reloaded, was refused when broken and reloaded when restored.
  - **Tests:** remounting a pack whose texture changed reloads it behind its handle (same slot, generation 1) and sends the material that binds it again, not the mesh; a material moved to another texture loads the new texture first and releases the old one; a texture and the material binding it, both changed, upload texture then material once; a corrupt block, an invalid material and a refused upload each keep the old version and report; an asset removed from its pack keeps its version, fails a new renderer, and loads again when it is back; changed unused assets are unloaded and assets mid-load start again with the new bytes; rigs reload without the GPU; a new renderer taking over a reload gets the new version and the rebound material as generation 1; remounting an unchanged, corrupt or conflicting pack changes nothing; a new texture two edited materials share that fails fails both their new versions; a failed character retried by a remount whose pack has fewer assets (so every entry moved) loads; a material's new version waiting on its texture's goes on when that edit is undone; an asset re-sent to a new renderer that changes meanwhile comes as a new generation. An independent review found the last four before they were fixed (two panics, a stall, a lost generation). A soak test remounts a pack between two versions (half the textures, a third of the materials' textures and a quarter of the meshes differ) over 300 rounds of loading and dropping under tight budgets, half the time undoing the edit before its reloads land: the app is never sent an older version than it holds, every reload it is told of is a generation it holds or has passed, and afterwards no slot, byte or GPU copy remains and live heap bytes are back at their baseline. Watching real directories: a burst of 30 writes over half a second is one change (once on an idle machine; since M1 A9 the test allows one more report per pause in the writes as long as the debounce, which it measures, and a few for stalled event delivery, as loaded CI runners make both); packs, shaders and documents are reported as such, the pack's temporary file and unrelated files not at all; removed and renamed packs are changes (since A9 checked to be reported, not to be reported alone: FSEvents may report files written just before a watch began, in a later batch), and a removed shader keeps its text and reports the error; builds without the backend watch nothing. The A4 test of the upload budget asserted a frame count that depends on the order decodes finish in; it now bounds what the order can't change.
  - **Cost:** idle, nothing measurable. Sandbox `--bench 3000` (1920×1080, 4× MSAA, Apple M5, release, `dev` builds, the watcher started on packs, shaders and the default day) against a `dev` build of c3ac568, two sets of three alternating rounds on a machine shared with four other builds: CPU p50 90.6, 93.2 and 94.1 µs against 90.6, 94.2 and 96.6 (the first set's first A8 round, 152.7 µs with a 6.5 ms p99, was a burst of outside load; its other rounds 96.6 and 94.2 against 97.7 and 99.5); the timed GPU frame 197, 256 and 260 µs against 199, 257 and 223; 305.3 allocations and 75,387 bytes per frame on both, so the per-frame poll allocates nothing. Without `dev`, CPU p50 92.3–92.8 µs against 91.3–92.0, the same 305.3 allocations and 74,738 bytes: the remount and generation bookkeeping costs nothing while no pack changes. A remount takes 0.1–0.3 ms of its frame (reading the header and table of contents, which waits on the IO thread, checking them and comparing the loaded assets); the new versions then load as any asset does.
- **As built (M1 W5, `sapphire_assets::probes`):** a new cooked asset type, the ambient probe grid (`ProbeGrid`, `.sprobe`, `AssetKind::ProbeGrid`, `ProbeGrid::VERSION` 1), rkyv read in place and checked whole on read (`ArchivedProbeGrid::validate`: placement, brick table and probe indices, coefficient counts, finite half floats; a grid without probes or kept bricks is refused, as is probe data with bits set past the validity bit, `ProbeData::UNUSED_BITS`, which the renderer keeps for its own use).
  - **Layout:** probes on a regular grid `spacing` meters apart from `origin`, in bricks of 4³ cells (`BRICK_CELLS`); a brick table maps every brick position of the grid's extent to a kept brick or `EMPTY_BRICK`, and a kept brick lists its 5³ probes, the ones on faces it shares with its neighbors included, so the renderer lays each brick out alone and filters within it; each probe's data is stored once.
  - **Per probe:** sky visibility as SH, L2 (9 coefficients) or L1 (4); per time-of-day key (`key_hours`, ascending), the bounce irradiance as L1 per channel (12), all IEEE half floats; and `ProbeData`: how far the probe sees along ±x, ±y, ±z in 31sts of the spacing, for the renderer's leak test, and whether it lies in open space.
  - It packs (A4) and reloads (A8) like any asset: the server reads it in place and hands the app the archived grid, which uploads it whole (`Renderer::set_probe_grid`, replacing the last); a test reloads one in place behind its handle, and the soak tests load and unload it with the rest.
- **As built (M1 X2 rehearsal, hot reload on the devices' builds):** `sapphire_hot_reload`'s watcher is now one type over a per-platform backend: `notify`'s where the crate has it (the desktops), and an uninhabited one elsewhere, whose `FileWatcher::new` reports `WatchError::Unsupported` and whose methods are a `match` on nothing. The reports the notify thread sends (`Changed`, `Errors`) and the channel they come through belong to the notify backend alone, so iOS and Android `dev` builds no longer warn about types they never build. The sandbox now logs each asset hot reload swaps in behind its handle (`asset swapped in`, with its ID, kind and generation), as live link already did for pushed ones (`pushed asset swapped in`), so a reload's timing can be read off the log; [m1-exit-run.md](m1-exit-run.md) has the timings measured that way.

- **As built (M1 W3, the fog card family drawn):** `FogCardMaterial` gains `facing` (`CardFacing::World`, the default, `Camera` or `Upright`), packed into `FogCardParams::modes[1]` (the block stays 80 bytes): `Material::VERSION` 5, and `Mesh::VERSION` 8 with it, since cooked meshes embed generated materials' blocks. Documents don't change version: the field defaults. The renderer draws the family on meshes (§5.12, "Environment", the W3 note).
- **As built (M1 W6, `sapphire_assets::{mesh, material::FoliageMaterial}`):** foliage's data (§5.12, "Environment", has the shader).
  - **The foliage stream** (`Mesh::VERSION` 10): an optional fourth vertex stream, `VertexStreams::foliage`, one 8-byte `VertexFoliage` per vertex: the three wind levels' weights (trunk sway, branch bend, leaf flutter) and the branch's phase as unorm8 × 4, the color gradient's coordinate as unorm8, three reserved bytes (error 1/510). `FoliageVertex` is the same at full precision, what the cooker computes; `VertexStreams::with_foliage` packs it, and `Mesh::read` checks the stream holds one entry per vertex or none, its reserved bytes zero (kept for M2's camera-facing leaf clusters). Meshes cooked without foliage settings leave it empty and are unchanged otherwise.
  - **Decision: a stream of its own, not the vertex color.** Vertex color RGB is the anime character family's data (AO, outline width, Z offset), and every family reads it; the wind and gradient data are foliage's alone, so the renderer keeps them in a buffer of their own that other meshes never pay for.
  - **`FoliageMaterial`** (`Material::VERSION` 7) gains `terminator_growth` (default 0.004 per meter, the environment's terminator remap), packed into `FoliageParams::shading.w`, with the canopy occlusion moved to `gradient_root.a` (the block stays 160 bytes). An `alpha_cutoff` of 0 now draws opaque: the material's flags leave `ALPHA_TEST` out, for trunks and solid canopies. The mask map's B (flutter weight) is unused: the cooker's per-vertex flutter weight replaces it. The documents' version is unchanged: the field defaults. W6 landed after C5, whose rim and hair settings took `Material::VERSION` 6 and `Mesh::VERSION` 9, so foliage's are 7 and 10; cooked data recooks rather than upgrades, so no layout is frozen.

**Tests**

- Load and unload soak tests checking for leaked handles.
- A pack-corruption test: a bad hash must be rejected.

### 5.11 `sapphire_rhi` (L2)

**How**

- **Decision:** don't write a full RHI trait now. `sapphire_rhi` re-exports wgpu 30 and adds engine helpers, and only `sapphire_rhi` and `sapphire_render` may touch wgpu. A future console backend then plugs in through wgpu's custom-backend hooks (expanded in v30) or by rewriting two crates, not the engine.
- **Device creation and feature tiers:** probe the adapter and fill a `GpuCaps` struct:
  - BC and ASTC texture support.
  - Timestamp queries, including whether they work inside passes.
  - Multi-draw indirect (MDI) and MDI-count.
  - Binding arrays (bindless).
  - `shader-f16`.
  - Pipeline-cache support.
  - Limits.
  - The renderer selects passes by `GpuCaps` plus the tier table, never by OS.
- **Uploads:** per-frame data goes through a `wgpu::util::StagingBelt`: mapped staging buffers reused across frames and copied in the frame's own encoder. `queue.write_buffer` creates a new staging buffer per call (it was most of the M0 frame's CPU time), so it's for one-off uploads such as meshes.
- **Transient pools:** textures and buffers pooled by descriptor and reused across frames. wgpu can't alias GPU memory, so pooling is the substitute.
- **Pipeline cache:** on Vulkan, create `wgpu::PipelineCache`, load it from `user://` at boot and save it at shutdown and after load screens.
- **Surfaces:**
  - Recreate on resize, on `SurfaceLost`, and on Android surface destroy/create.
  - Present mode: FIFO on mobile; FIFO by default on desktop, with Mailbox / Immediate as user options.
  - HDR surfaces come *later*.
- **Debug:**
  - Label every resource.
  - GPU pass timings via `wgpu-profiler` 0.28. **As built (M1 F5):** the frame graph times its passes with its own timestamp queries instead (§5.12, debug views; why in §5.20).
  - A capture hotkey that calls wgpu's `start_graphics_debugger_capture` (RenderDoc, Xcode or PIX).

### 5.12 `sapphire_render` (L3)

The big one. It owns everything from `FramePacket` to pixels.

**Frame graph**

- Rebuild the graph in code each frame (cheap). Each pass declares the virtual resources it reads and writes. Compile does this:
  1. Cull passes whose outputs are unused.
  2. Order the passes.
  3. Compute resource lifetimes.
  4. Assign textures from the transient pool.
- Record passes on workers into separate `CommandEncoder`s, then submit them in order in one `queue.submit`. wgpu tracks barriers, so the graph's job is ordering, culling, pooling and parallel recording.
- Choose passes per tier, from the data table in "Quality tiers" below.
- **As built (M1 R1, `sapphire_render::graph`):**
  - Passes run in the order they're added; compile checks every read follows a write.
  - A write replaces a texture's contents, so a pass that keeps them (`LoadOp::Load`, partial draws) also declares a read. Culling relies on this.
  - Transients with equal descriptors and disjoint lifetimes share one pooled texture; the pool (`sapphire_rhi::TransientPool`) matches textures across frames and evicts ones unused for 60 frames.
  - Under 4 running passes, recording stays on the calling thread in one encoder; from 4, it splits into encoders of at least 2 passes on the job pool.
  - Cost: about 10 heap allocations per frame (node lists, one boxed closure per pass) and under 1 µs of CPU for M0's two passes. Uploads are side-effect passes added first. Buffers stay outside the graph (M1 R2's note, "Skinning and morph targets", says why).
- **As built (M1 W2):** the sky adds a side-effect pass, `sky cube`, before the main pass: one face of the persistent sky cube (§5.12, "Sky"), drawn through that face's view, so a reader added after it sees this frame's face. It is timed like the main pass. The main pass draws the sky where it did, after the opaque surfaces and outlines and before transparents, through the sky's own pipeline variants.

**Scene and draws**

- **Persistent GPU scene:** an instance buffer (transforms, material IDs) and a material parameter buffer (storage buffer indexed by material ID), updated from the extract deltas.
- **Mesh pool:** large shared vertex and index buffers with offsets, which means fewer rebinds and makes MDI possible later.
- **Culling:**
  - v1: CPU frustum, distance and small-object culling, parallel per view (main camera and each shadow cascade).
  - Later, on desktop: GPU Hi-Z occlusion culling plus MDI.
- **Sorting:** opaque draws by pipeline → material → mesh, with instancing. Transparents back to front per emitter or object.
- **As built (M1 F3, `sapphire_render::scene`):**
  - Instances live in slots of the instance buffer, found by `InstanceId` (a hash map with a one-multiply hasher) and reused through a free list. A CPU mirror holds every slot's GPU data. A packet rewrites only the slots it touches, and the uploads pass writes those through the staging belt in contiguous runs.
  - Draw records name their instance by slot. The draw list is therefore rebuilt, and its records uploaded, only when instances are added or removed, change mesh, or meshes are uploaded; a move costs one 112-byte upload (176 bytes since M1 R2, plus a second upload once the instance comes to rest; see that package's note). `RenderStats` counts changed instances and draw-list builds.
  - A grown instance buffer is filled by a GPU copy of the old one, so instances uploaded earlier aren't sent again.
  - Draws stay in slot order. Sorting comes with a second opaque pipeline, and costs nothing on frames that only move instances, since the list is rebuilt only when the set changes.
- **As built (M1 W4):** a second material family draws on meshes, so draws now sort by pipeline: the draw list groups them by position format and surface variant (a coverage, and the anime character shader for a part or the environment shader), whatever order instances and submeshes come in, and families mix freely within a mesh. Within a group they keep slot order; sorting by material and mesh stays later work. "Environment" below has the details.

**Skinning and morph targets**

- A compute pass skins every visible skinned instance once per frame into a per-instance vertex buffer, applying blendshape (face) weights in the same dispatch.
- The prepass, shadow, opaque and outline passes all reuse that buffer, so a character is skinned once rather than four times.
- **As built (M1 A5, `sapphire_render::{mesh_pool, pipelines}`, `shaders/frame.wgsl`):**
  - The vertex shaders decode the cooked streams (§5.10) as uploaded: `decode_vertex` in `frame.wgsl`, called by the anime character and outline vertex shaders. Vertex slots are 0 the draw record, 1 positions, 2 shading, 3 surface, so a position-only pass (shadows, R2's prepass) binds slots 0 and 1 alone.
  - The draw record grows from 8 to 48 bytes to carry its mesh's dequantization (position offset and scale, UV transform). Decoding then costs no binding: a mesh table would have been a third vertex-stage storage buffer against the budget in the render crate docs. f32-position meshes use the same shaders with an identity transform; only slot 1's attribute differs, and their pipelines are built when the first one is uploaded.
  - The mesh pool keeps one slab of stream buffers per position format, each with a single vertex numbering, as base-vertex multi-draw-indirect will need; indices and materials are shared. Uploads copy the archive's streams as they lie. Every LOD's indices are uploaded and LOD0 is drawn until LOD selection lands with culling. The cook merges submeshes by material, so the mannequin draws 5 times per pass instead of 9.
  - **Skinned meshes draw in bind pose**, morph targets at weight 0. Influences and morph deltas stay in the cooked mesh, already in the storage layouts R2 needs. R2 uploads them, builds palettes as `model_from_joint × inverse_bind_matrix` per palette entry, decodes positions to f32 before adding deltas (morphed and skinned positions leave the bind-pose bounds), and writes per-instance f32 positions (slot 1, drawn through the f32 pipelines with an identity dequantization) and `VertexShading` (slot 2), keeping the pool's surface stream.
  - Golden images move by mean 0.0001 and p99 0.0018 FLIP on Metal (thresholds 0.01 and 0.3), so the goldens are unchanged. A GPU test renders a sphere with unorm16 and with f32 positions: 4 of 76,800 pixels differ by more than 8/255.
  - Cost against M1 R3 (sandbox `--bench 3000`, 1920×1080, 4× MSAA, Apple M5, release, three alternating rounds): CPU p50 90.2–90.6 µs against 90.9–91.2, GPU wait 483–512 µs against 486–525, both within noise; 298.3 allocations per frame against 294.3: the renderer's own per-frame code allocates nothing new, so the four are wgpu's, presumably for binding four vertex buffers instead of two (not traced further). The mesh pool holds 229,616 bytes against 251,696, with every LOD's indices.
- **As built (M1 R2, `sapphire_render::{skinning, mesh_pool, draw}`, `shaders/skinning.wgsl`, `shaders/prepass.wgsl`):**
  - **Deformable meshes.** A mesh with a skin or morph targets is deformable. Its streams, influences and morph deltas go to one deformable buffer (storage and vertex) instead of a slab.
    - The upload regroups morph deltas by vertex with a counting sort: per vertex, its deltas in target order, each holding its target index. The skinning pass then applies morphs in the same dispatch that skins, each vertex walking only its own deltas, with no atomics and no pass per target.
    - Every instance of a deformable mesh owns outputs in one shared buffer (first-fit ranges of vertices): f32 positions twice and `VertexShading` normals, 32 bytes a vertex. Every pass draws it from those through the f32 pipelines with an identity dequantization (slots 1 and 2), plus the mesh's own surface stream (slot 3). Static meshes are untouched and cost nothing extra.
  - **One dispatch per frame, not one per instance.** The outputs are one read-write storage buffer, which wgpu fences between dispatches, so per-instance dispatches would run one after another and leave the GPU idle on small instances.
    - Each instance takes a run of 64-thread workgroups and an 80-byte job (offsets, flags, dequantization) in a per-frame buffer, along with its palette (three rows per matrix) and dense weights. A workgroup finds its job by binary search.
    - Per vertex, the pass decodes the bind pose to f32 and adds the weighted morph deltas. The binary16 deltas are decoded by hand, because naga accepts `unpack2x16float` only with the f16-in-f32 capability. It then blends four palette matrices by their unorm8 weights and transforms the position, the normal and the outline normal (normals by the blended linear part, renormalized).
    - The outline normal isn't morphed, so the inverted hull stays closed.
  - **What a frame skins.** Only instances posed since the last recorded frame, plus new ones in their bind pose. Poses from frames that were prepared but not recorded collapse into the latest. Only the drawn LOD's vertex prefix is skinned: LOD0 until LOD selection.
  - **Velocity.**
    - An instance's positions are double-buffered: a frame that skins it writes the other half, so the last drawn pose is still there for velocity. Re-skinning the previous frame from last frame's palette would read the bind pose, influences, palette and every morph delta a second time, and would need last frame's weights too. The second half costs 12 bytes a vertex of memory and one 12-byte read instead.
    - An instance not skinned in a frame binds one half twice, so it has no motion.
    - Instances keep the transform they were last drawn with: `InstanceData` grows to 176 bytes, and a moved instance is uploaded once more when it comes to rest. The view keeps its last view-projection.
  - **Prepasses.** They run after skinning and before the main pass, single-sampled, at the scene's size (the target's at the render scale, M1 R6), and `Renderer::record` returns them as `PrepassTargets`.
    - The character prepass writes the depth of characters (skinned and morphed instances) alone, position-only, on every tier.
    - The scene prepass writes every opaque instance's depth, world normals (octahedral `Rg16Float`) and velocity (`Rg16Float`, motion in texture coordinates). Pixels no geometry covers stay 0, for TAA to reproject from depth.
    - Every pass decodes positions through one function and marks its clip position invariant, so the depths match.
    - Nothing reads them yet, so the frame graph culls both: two boxed closures a frame, and no GPU time. The outline hull's velocity comes with C4.
  - **Decision: buffers stay outside the frame graph** (R1 had planned them for R2). Every buffer is persistent, compute skinning's outputs included, so declaring them would never cull a pass, and wgpu already orders accesses to them. Passes that write buffers stay side effects added before their readers. Buffer resources wait for the first transient buffers.
  - **Bindings.** The skinning pass binds three storage buffers in its own group 0 (sources, frame data, outputs), within the floor's four. The view uniforms gain the previous view-projection and the instance buffer the previous transform. See the crate docs, "Binding budget".
  - **Stats** (`RenderStats::skinning`, rule R9): instances; the instances, vertices and palette bytes the frame skins; output bytes; ignored poses; and instances whose outputs don't fit. The output buffer is a single storage binding, 128 MiB at the device floor, which is about 4 million vertices. The pass's GPU time is the graph's `skinning` timing. The overlay shows a skinning row.
  - **Sandbox:** `--skinned` puts a stalk beside the character: a 1.5 m procedural capsule of 2,145 vertices, six bones with tent weights, and two sparse morph targets (`bulb` and `pinch`) with exact normal deltas. It sways and breathes on its entity clock. The golden scene `turntable-skinned` shows it (Metal golden only; lavapipe and WARP come from CI). The four earlier goldens are unchanged (mean ≤ 0.0001). The iOS Simulator runs it at 60 fps.
  - **Tests (Metal):**
    - The GPU outputs match a CPU reference within 1.6e-7 relative position error and 0.06° of normal. The reference covers random palettes (scale 0.5–2), 1–4 influences, u8 and u16 indices, unorm16 and f32 positions and three sparse morph targets.
    - An instance never posed draws exactly like the unskinned mesh. With an identity palette, no pixel differs by more than 8 steps.
    - Posed skins and morphs draw like the mesh deformed on the CPU and drawn static: no pixel differs by more than 8 steps.
    - The scene prepass's depth equals the main pass's bit for bit. The character prepass's equals it wherever nothing stands in front.
    - Velocity of a moved static square and a lifted skinned one matches the projection within f16 precision, is exactly 0 once they hold still, and follows a camera move.
    - There are no validation errors from 1×1 to 17×1000, with MSAA off and 4×. Outputs survive buffer growth and reuse freed ranges, and bad poses are ignored.
  - **Cost** against M1 F5 (sandbox `--bench 3000`, 1920×1080, 4× MSAA, Apple M5, release, three alternating rounds):
    - Without `--skinned`: CPU p50 91.5–91.6 µs against 90.9–92.0, unchanged; p99 142–152 µs against 125–155. 303.3 allocations per frame against 299.3: the two culled prepasses' closures and longer graph lists. GPU wait within noise.
    - With `--skinned`: CPU p50 100–103 µs, about 10 µs more, for recording the skinning pass, the stalk's draws and its two `Extract` systems. 320.4 allocations per frame, 17 more (the pass's closure, wgpu's compute pass and the systems; not traced further). The skinning pass takes 4–9 µs of GPU time for 2,145 vertices (timestamps; the main pass takes 80 µs).
    - Rebased onto M1 R6 (0cd857e), two more alternating rounds against its head agree: without `--skinned`, p50 92.1–92.8 µs against 92.1–92.5 and the same 4 more allocations; with it, p50 101–102 µs and 8–10 µs of GPU time for skinning.

**Lighting**

- **Desktop:** clustered forward+. A compute pass bins lights into a 16×9×24 grid.
- **Mobile:** per-object light lists, with at most 4 local lights per object.
- **Sun:** cascaded shadows. Desktop has 4 cascades (far cascades cached and updated in turns); mobile has 2.
- **Character shadow (desktop):** a separate high-resolution per-character shadow frustum for crisp contact shadows.
  - **M1 C2 (scope):** characters receiving environment casters only, the per-character ground shadow map on desktop and the capsule shadow on mobile belong to R4 (M1 plan), under the contract in C2's note below ("Anime character shading").
- **Ambient:**
  - A per-zone probe grid: sky visibility as SH plus bounce baked per time-of-day key (static per-zone probes go wrong as soon as the sun moves).
  - Reflection probes are relit at runtime on desktop and Mobile-High, and static on Mobile-Low (Genshin's approach).
  - Reflection probes: BC6H on desktop; RGBA16F or RGB9E5 at small sizes on mobile.
  - Capsule AO for characters on all tiers; GTAO on desktop.
- **As built (M1 R5, `sapphire_render::lights`):**
  - `RenderSettings::light_path` picks clustered forward+ or per-object lists, and `max_lights` caps the lights a frame draws (clamped to `MAX_LIGHTS`, 1024). `RenderSettings::for_device` gives desktops the clustered path with 64 lights and phones per-object lists with 8 (§3.4), until quality tiers (R6) choose per tier. The app passes the device class; the renderer has no `cfg` (rule R7). Both paths run on every backend, so both are tested on desktop.
  - Each frame the CPU drops lights that can't light anything and those whose range sphere misses the main view's frustum (five planes; the far plane is at infinity), keeps the `max_lights` nearest the camera, in packet order, and packs them at 64 bytes each.
  - **Clustered (desktop):** 16 × 9 screen tiles by 24 view-depth slices. Slice 0 runs from the camera to 1 m; slices 1–23 split 1 m to 500 m exponentially (about 31% deeper each), and the last continues to infinity, matching the infinite far plane. A pixel takes its slice from its reverse-Z depth (view depth = near / z). A compute pass tests each light against each cluster's box (since the WARP light-cost fix below, two dispatches without workgroup memory: one thread per light moves the lights into view space, then one thread per cluster, 128 per workgroup, walks them): the range sphere against the box, then, for spots narrower than 90°, the cone against a sphere around the box (Wronski's cone test). Lists come out in index order, at most `min(max_lights, 64)` per cluster, in one buffer at a fixed stride: a count, then 16-bit indices two to a word (456 KB at 64). A full cluster keeps the packet's earliest lights; at the default budgets none can overflow. The pass is a side effect between the uploads and the main pass (buffers aren't graph resources yet), and runs only on frames with lights. On 400 random points and spots, the GPU lists match the CPU reference exactly in all 3,456 clusters, 166 of them full.
  - **Per object (mobile):** each instance lists its four most influential lights: luminance × range window × cone at the point of its bounding sphere nearest the light. Ties go to the lower index, and the list holds them in index order, as clusters do. Lists are chosen again for instances added, moved or re-meshed, or for every instance when the lights changed, and only the ones that changed are uploaded, in runs of slots. They use the cluster lists' layout (3 words each) in the same binding, indexed by the instance slot that the vertex stage passes as a flat varying. Bounding spheres come from the cooked mesh bounds.
  - **Shading (`anime_character.wgsl`):** each listed light is lit in the sun's two-tone model. Its N·L is split at the material's threshold plus the ILM bias into a lit and a shadow tone, with the same authored shadow color, in the light's color × its range window × its spot cone. The window is `(1 − d²/r²)²` (no inverse-square hot spot; intensity 1 is as bright as a unit sun), and the cone a smoothstep in cos θ from the outer to the inner angle. The one-pixel edge comes from N·L's exact screen derivative per light, built from dN and dP taken once per pixel (derivatives aren't allowed in the per-pixel loop). The summed light is held under a luminance of 2, so stacked lights tint rather than blow out (the reference games' `_FilterLight`). Local lights add no rim. A frame without lights skips all of it on a uniform branch, and the three earlier goldens stayed bit-identical. In a GPU test, a red light beside a sun-shadowed sphere splits it within two pixels of the predicted terminator, at the material's 2:1 tone ratio (0.19 to 0.40 of added red across the edge).
  - The two paths draw identical pixels for up to four lights (a GPU test, and the `turntable-lights` scene). `RenderStats::lights` counts lights drawn, culled and dropped, the clusters binned, the list capacity and per-object lists chosen.
  - Bindings: group 0 gains the light buffer and the list buffer, both fragment-only, so the fragment stage uses 3 of the 4 storage buffers Vulkan 1.1 phones guarantee and the vertex stage still 2. The cluster pass binds the same buffers in its own group 0, and the view group as group 1 (crate docs, "Binding budget").
  - Cost against M1 R3 (sandbox `--bench 3000` at 1920×1080 with 4× MSAA, Apple M5, release, two sets of three alternating rounds):
    - Without lights: CPU p50 89–96 µs against R3's 88–94 µs, so no measurable change; 295.3 allocations per frame against 294.3 (the second `Extract` system, §4.6).
    - With the three demo lights, clustered: p50 95–101 µs, about 5 µs more, which is recording the compute pass; 298.3 allocations (its boxed closure and wgpu's compute pass); 13 graph passes instead of 12.
    - With them per object: p50 89–93 µs, 295.3 allocations and 12 passes. The lists are chosen again only for the turntable, the one instance that moves, and upload nothing while they stay the same.
    - p99 was 105–272 µs in every configuration, the baseline's included, and the mean GPU wait stayed within its noise (about 465–490 µs).
- **As built (M1, the WARP light-cost fix: `lights::clusters`, `shaders/clusters.wgsl`):**
  - **Cause:** on WARP (the Windows CI's CPU rasterizer, 1.0.21) every golden scene with local lights took about 76 s longer: `turntable-lights` 115 s against `turntable`'s 12 s for one frame, `environment-night` and `fog-night` about 119 s against 22 s. Timed per pipeline and per pass (a throwaway CI branch, run 40426024233): creating the cluster pass's compute pipeline took 5.5–6.1 s in every desktop process, lights or not, and its first dispatch 69–72 s, at 1, 3 or 200 lights; later frames took 123–134 ms. It was the shader's `var<workgroup>` batches and `workgroupBarrier`: one batch with its barrier outside any loop cost the same, and so did turning off naga's loop bounding. Lavapipe compiled and ran it in milliseconds, and the per-object path (no compute pass) never paid.
  - **Fix:** the pass is two dispatches in one compute pass, with no workgroup memory and no barrier. `cs_cluster_lights` (one thread per drawn light, 64 per workgroup) writes the lights in view space to the pass's own buffer (32 bytes a light, `max_lights` of them, group 0 binding 3 of its own layout); `cs_clusters` (one thread per cluster) walks them as it walked the batches. The math is the same, so the lists are: the GPU test still matches the CPU reference in all 3,456 clusters at 400 lights, and every golden is byte-identical on Metal; the debug run's barrier-free captures matched the shipped ones exactly on WARP and lavapipe. Hot reload rebuilds both pipelines (`ClusterPipelines`), so a `frame.wgsl` edit rebuilds one more pipeline than before (11 programs and 14 or 18 pipelines in `tests/hot_reload.rs`, with C6's and F5's).
  - **On WARP** a barrier-free cluster pipeline takes 35–44 ms to create and its first run 24 ms. In the fix's CI run against one on its base (439c5fc, the same four shards): `turntable-lights` 3.0 s against 47.0 s, `environment-night` 14.4 against 58.5, `fog-night` 14.8 against 61.1, `lookdev-town-hero` 32.6 against 80.2, and every other desktop scene 6–15 s less; the 57 scenes besides W5's six ambient ones took 715 s against 1,402 s (1,343–1,577 s in three other runs that day). The ambient scenes vary between runs on the same code far more than that (`ambient-day` 263–668 s, `ambient-night` 250–1,185 s), so they show nothing either way. Lavapipe's times didn't move (462 s for the 57 in both runs). Not every barrier is slow on WARP: temporal AA's resolve, one barrier and then reads of a fixed neighborhood, takes about 150 ms to create.
  - **Cost on an Apple M5** (sandbox `--bench 120` at 1920×1080, release, the light-cluster pass's GPU time, minima of 8–12 alternating rounds against b6f5cce; 253 lights by raising `max_lights` in a local measurement build): Desktop-High turntable 3 lights 10.9–11.0 µs against 6.8–9.3, 64 lights 17.4 against 20.4–20.7, 253 lights 42.8–45.8 against 45.0–50.5; the night courtyard 3 lights 6.4 against 7.1–7.8, 253 lights 43.7–44.1 against 46.7–49.6; Mobile-High on the clustered path (it ships per-object) 3 lights 12.2–13.1 against 12.6–15.2, 253 lights 42.8 and 50.7 against 43.8–44.2 and 52.6–53.2. Whole-frame GPU time and CPU time didn't move. The few microseconds at 3 lights on the turntable are the binning waiting for the first dispatch on an otherwise idle GPU.
  - **Rejected:** moving every light into view space in every binning thread (one dispatch) cost 5–52 µs more at 253 lights; the CPU writing the view-space lights each frame cost 17 µs more at 253 lights in the courtyard, where the walk starts cold in the cache; a shared batch of the first 128 lights behind one barrier outside the loop is as slow on WARP as before.
- **As built (M1 R4, `sapphire_render::shadows`, the receivers in `frame.wgsl`, `shaders/shadows.wgsl`):**
  - **Decision: the frame's character list classifies casters and receivers.** An instance listed in `FramePacket::characters` with a valid entry is a character this frame; every other instance is environment. No flag or layer: the renderer already resolves each instance's character entry per frame (`InstanceData::character`, C2), and the shaders branch on the same index, so casting and receiving can't disagree. Environment instances cast into the cascades and receive the cascades and every character's own shadow; characters cast only their own shadow and receive the cascades alone, so a character never shadows itself (C2's contract), and characters don't shadow each other (optional in the contract; later).
  - **Cascades:** `cascades` layers of one `Depth32Float` array, reverse-Z (1 toward the light, cleared to 0), out to `distance`, split by the practical scheme (80% logarithmic). Per tier (`tiers.ron`): Desktop-High 4 × 2048² to 120 m, Desktop-Low 4 × 1024² to 100 m, Mobile-High 2 × 1024² to 50 m, Mobile-Low 2 × 1024² to 40 m.
  - **Stability:** the key light is quantized (its shadow slope rounded to 5 mm per meter of height, with the sun kept 3° above the horizon), so a running day leaves the maps exactly still between steps; each cascade covers the sphere around its slice of the view frustum, whose size never changes as the camera turns (radius rounded up to 1/16 m), centered on whole texels of light space, so the texel grid stays fixed to the world. Shadow views are never jittered; temporal AA jitters the main view alone.
  - **Caching:** the farthest `cached_cascades` are drawn in turns and otherwise sampled as last drawn, with the matrices they were drawn with: on desktop the far two alternate (each every other frame), on phones the far one is drawn every other frame. All are drawn again when the quantized light steps, on a camera cut and after a frame without a view; a cached one early once the view has moved an eighth of its width. A cascade nothing casts into is drawn once, empty, and then left as it is until something does.
  - **Casters:** the depth-writing surfaces (opaque and alpha-tested, drawn solid) of environment instances, through one depth-only pipeline per position format (positions alone, two-sided, a slope-scaled raster bias), culled per cascade by their bounding spheres; skinned and morphed environment instances, whose posed bounds aren't known yet, go into every cascade. Transparent surfaces and outline hulls cast nothing. Mesh bounds moved into the mesh records (the cooked bounds as a sphere), which the per-object light lists and C4's outline cutoff read too.
  - **Billboards and impostors** (W6) face the light in the shadow passes with no code of their own: their vertex shaders orient cards by the bound view's axes (`ViewUniforms::view_from_world`), never by the main camera, and each shadow view carries the light's axes.
  - **Receivers** (`frame.wgsl`): `shadow_visibility()` for environment surfaces, `cascade_visibility()` for characters, both the key light's visibility (1 lit, 0 shadowed). A point takes the first cascade whose square holds it with room for the filter, blends into the next over a cascade's outer tenth, and shadows fade out over the last tenth of the distance. **Bias:** the lookup moves off the surface along its geometric normal by half a texel plus half the filter's width × sin θ (where the light grazes the surface), then toward the light by a quarter texel; casters carry a constant and slope-scaled raster bias. A lit plane never shadows itself under the filter, from a light 70° up down to 6°, and a box's shadow meets its foot with no lit gap at 1.1 cm pixels (the GPU tests).
  - **Decision: a tent PCF, not Poisson.** 5 × 5 texels in 9 bilinear compare taps on desktop, 3 × 3 in 4 on phones (`ShadowFilter`, Castaño 2013), weights normalized so an unoccluded point is exactly 1. It is a smooth field without noise, identical every frame and stable under temporal AA, which receivers threshold (the anime character shader's light split, the environment's terminator) into a clean edge along a smooth contour through the texels. A rotated Poisson disc needs temporal AA to hide its noise, which phones don't run.
  - **Tint:** the visibility enters each receiver's split as occlusion, in half-Lambert space, so a shadowed point takes the shadow side's tones: the environment's terminator keeps W1's shadow tint of the key light there (W4), and character surfaces go into their authored shade tones (C1). Shadows are colored, never black.
  - **Characters' own shadows.** Desktop (`character_shadow`): the characters nearest the camera, at most 8, each draw a map: an orthographic frustum from the same quantized light around their body and their instances' bounds, padded and texel-snapped, `character_resolution`² (1024 on Desktop-High, 512 on Desktop-Low), tiles of one 4 × 2 atlas drawn in one pass with a viewport per tile. A map reaches 40 m past the character away from the light, fading over the last quarter. Phones, and characters past the desktop's maps: the body's capsule casts analytically (the closest approach of the light's ray from the receiver to the capsule's axis, against the radius softened by 35% at contact and 3 cm per meter from it, gone 8 m away), at most 16 capsules, nearest first. No map, no pass.
  - **Faces** never read a shadow map (C3's contract): a compute pass (`face shadows`, `shadows.wgsl`) samples the cascades at seven points of a sphere 0.12 m around each character's head once a frame, in frames where a face draws, and writes the visibility into the character's entry (`CharacterData::shadow`, appended; entries grow from 128 to 160 bytes). The face permutation takes it as occlusion of its split input, with its always-lit eyes and brows, so an environment shadow reaches the face whole.
  - **Passes** are side effects before the main pass, since the maps are persistent textures outside the frame graph (as the sky cube is): `shadow cascade 0`… for each cascade drawn, `character shadows` and `face shadows`. The shadows' views follow the sky cube's six in the view buffer. Receivers read group 0: `ShadowUniforms` at binding 4, the cascades at 5, the atlas at 6 and a comparison sampler at 7, fragment-only; no storage buffer is added. The caster pipelines bind the view group and the anime character family's scene group (as the prepasses do) and leave group 0 out, which holds the maps they draw into.
  - **Settings:** `RenderSettings::shadows: Option<ShadowSettings>`, from the tier (see "Quality tiers"). `None` draws none: 1 × 1 maps, no passes, and the shaders' `SHADOWS` pipeline constant off, so they compile to what they were without shadows. The player's setting (`QualitySettings::shadows`, the sandbox's `--shadows on|off`) turns them off.
  - **Stats** (rule R9): `RenderStats::shadows` (`ShadowStats`): cascades and their resolution, cascades drawn and cached this frame, character maps, capsules and characters casting nothing of their own, caster draws and the environment draws culled from cascades, and the light's steps. The overlay's stats panel shows them, and its GPU budgets a "Shadows" row: the shadow passes' summed time (F5's pass timer) against §3.3's 2.0 ms (desktop) or 3.0 ms (phones). The bench logs the same time.
  - **Golden images:** the twenty-six earlier scenes (W4's three, P2's three post scenes and C4's four outline scenes included) pin shadows off (`--shadows off`), beside P2's `--plain-post` where they have it, and their Metal captures are unchanged, byte for byte. Six new scenes, Metal goldens only, each pinning its tier and plain post (`--plain-post`, R3's code), so they show shadows alone: `shadows-contact` (Desktop-High, the stage's floor: the character's map casts a contact shadow onto its pedestal, split off as environment, and on across the floor; the figure shows no mark of its own shadow), `shadows-pillar` (a stone pillar toward the sun: its shadow crosses the character at the shoulders, a clean edge in its authored shade tones, and the pedestal diagonally; no acne on the pillar, floor or figure), `shadows-faces` (the face rig at 14:00 behind a pillar: the four heads it covers go wholly into their shade tones, eyes and brows with them, while the others keep exactly their face-map shading, against the same frame with shadows off), `shadows-courtyard` (W4's courtyard at 10:00 under shadows: lanterns, boulders and the character casting onto the ground, tinted), `shadows-sunset` (the courtyard at 18:00: the pillars' and lanterns' shadows long across the ground into the far cascades, tinted, clean-edged; by 18:30 the wall's shadow already covers the colonnade) and `shadows-capsule` (Mobile-Low: two cascades, the 3 × 3 tent and 4× MSAA, the character's capsule a soft blob around its feet running on across the floor). All assessed visually at 640×360 and the stage scenes at 1280×720.
  - **Sandbox:** `--stage floor|pillar` (`stage.rs`) stands the character on a 64 m floor and, with `pillar`, a stone pillar toward the key light at the starting hour, sized so its shadow covers the subject up to its chest (or every head of `--faces`' rig); floor and pillar draw with the environment family. The mannequin's pedestal is part of its mesh, so on the stage the character draws the mesh without its `Pedestal` slot's submeshes and the pedestal is an environment instance of its own (the server keeps no mesh bytes after upload, so the content keeps a copy of the character's mesh with `--stage`).
  - **Tests (Metal, `tests/shadows.rs`, 14):** lit planes never shadow themselves on either tier class under five lights from 70° down to 6°; a box's shadow meets its foot with no lit gap and ends within 3 pixels of where the light puts it, its sides within 2; moving the camera 4 pixels moves every ground pixel, shadows included, by exactly 4 (snapping); a sub-step turn of the sun changes no pixel and a step redraws every cascade; the box's shadow holds in every cascade out to the distance, and is gone past it, on both tier classes; cached cascades take their turns (3 drawn of 4 each frame), draw the same frame as when fresh, and hold the shadow while the camera drifts; a character never shadows itself, the environment shadows a character, and a character shadows the environment through its map (desktop) and capsule (phones), one map per body; a floating character's own shadow lands where the light puts it on both tier classes, and none without a body; a face takes a wall's shadow whole where a body splits along its edge; a lit outline goes into a slab's shadow with its sphere, at the shade tone's share of its lit tone; with nothing casting, shadows on draw the same tones as off (at most 4 edge pixels round differently); the environment shader's ground keeps the frame's shadow tint in an environment caster's and a character's shadow, on both tier classes, under two tints; converged under temporal AA, a shadowed scene changes by a mean of 0.013 steps from frame to frame, with 51 of 65,536 pixels over 2 (limits 0.06 and 163); a `shadows.wgsl` edit rebuilds the face pass and the casters built so far, a `frame.wgsl` edit seven programs, and a renamed entry point is refused while the shadows draw on. Twelve mutations each fail a test: no texel snapping, no sun quantization, characters casting into the cascades, faces sampling the maps per pixel, no normal offset, characters receiving their own maps, no caching, capsules or character maps casting nothing, no distance fade, the environment's hook left lit, and lines ignoring cast shadows. Headless (`shadows::fit` and `shadows::plan`, 16): quantization, the light space's frame, slice spheres, splits, projections, snapping that ignores the camera's turn, character frusta clear of their tile's edges, classification, maps for the nearest and capsules for the rest, phones' capsules, turn-taking and early redraws, empty cascades, per-cascade culling, frames without a view; the WGSL structs match Rust field by field, and every surface variant of both families specializes and validates with shadows off and under either filter.
  - **Cost** (sandbox `--bench 3000` and its 239 GPU-timed frames, 1920×1080, Apple M5, release, against a release build of W4's head, c4adfd7: six interleaved rounds at load average 3–6, after a first set of three alternating rounds under load 15–23 that agreed within its noise; GPU times are the passes' mean exclusive times):
    - **Desktop-High, the courtyard** (`--environment`: three cascades drawn a frame and one cached, the character's map, 20 caster draws): the shadow passes take 252–267 µs (513–564 µs from first start to last end, overlapping the prepasses), and receiving them grows the main pass from 313–352 to 958–987 µs, about 0.65 ms of tent lookups over two million pixels. That is about 0.9 ms in all against §3.3's 2.0 ms for 4 cascades; the receivers' share lands in the main pass, which stays under a third of forward opaque's 3.5 ms (shadows module docs, "Later milestones", for cheaper receivers). The GPU frame is 2,145–2,286 µs against 1,326–1,440. CPU p50 147–165 µs against 121–130 (planning, and recording four more passes and their draws); 500.2 allocations a frame against 412.7: one per staged upload, the rest wgpu recording the passes and the frame graph's command buffers (7 instead of 6). The renderer's own per-frame shadow code allocates nothing: the plan reuses its vectors.
    - **Desktop-High, the default scene** (no environment, so every cascade is drawn empty once and then left; the character's map alone): the shadow passes take 79–92 µs, mostly clearing the 4096 × 2048 atlas, and the main pass 347–380 µs against 274–311; CPU p50 130–141 µs against 118–130; 473.8 allocations against 407.7, the graph grouping its 18 passes into 9 command buffers instead of 6. Leaving empty cascades undrawn and the face pass out of frames without faces took this scene from 520.2 allocations and 22 passes to that.
    - **Mobile-Low's settings** (1049×590, 4× MSAA, two cascades of 1024², the 3 × 3 tent, capsules): in the courtyard one cascade is drawn a frame, 30–39 µs, and receiving grows the main pass from 111–122 to 223–258 µs, about 0.15 ms in all against 3.0 ms; CPU p50 101–109 µs against 90–95; 304.9 allocations against 270.4. In the default scene no shadow pass runs (empty cascades, the capsule analytic): the main pass takes 101–125 µs against 84–89, CPU p50 91–98 µs against 90–93, and 265.4 allocations against 264.4 (the uniforms' upload). Phones are measured in X1.
    - **Shadows off** is the base: in the courtyard CPU p50 119–129 µs, the GPU frame 1,338–1,413 µs and the main pass 313–330 µs, with W4's 412.7 allocations.
    - **Re-measured on P2 and C4** (Desktop-High's courtyard, six interleaved rounds each against release builds of 564a3a3 and f3cde97): the shadow passes take 265–319 and 246–265 µs, and the main pass grows from 316–381 to 998–1,060 µs and from 307–314 to 966–997 µs, so receiving still costs about 0.65–0.7 ms; 478.2 and 484.2 allocations against 390.7 and 396.7, the same 87.5 more; shadows off is the base on both. One C4 round with shadows on, slowed about 1.6× throughout by other work on the machine, is left out.

**Anime character shading**

One uber-shader family, with at most 8 static permutation bits: layers, alpha-test, outline, face, hair, eye, transparent, and one free (skinned and morph, the first plan's, became the f32 position pipelines in M1 R2, and M1 C7 spent one on the material layers). Everything else is a uniform branch. M1's track C ([m1-plan.md](m1-plan.md)) refines what follows:

- **Two diffuse modes:** ramp rows (Genshin, Star Rail, WuWa), or analytic bands with per-ID colors (ZZZ, cheapest on mobile).
- **Lighting:** cast shadows push pixels into the shadow band. Characters receive only environment casters and never self-shadow; the ground gets a per-character shadow.
- **Stylized character GI:** spherized, camera-biased normals, plus an HSV clamp.
- **A character-only depth prepass on every tier** feeds rim light, hair shadow on the face, and eyes over the bangs.
- **Faces** get a vertical SDF channel for light from above.
- **Metals** use a matcap, plus a reflection add when metallic > 0.9.

- **Two-tone light split:** N·L is thresholded into light and shadow. Painted ILM / lightmap masks (specular mask, shadow bias, specular threshold, material ID) shift the threshold.
- **Ramps:**
  - Shadow color comes from a ramp texture array, with the row picked by material ID and a day/night ramp pair.
  - Ramps stay uncompressed, because block compression bands them.
- **Faces:**
  - An SDF face-shadow map is sampled from the light direction projected into head space.
  - Each character's head-forward and head-up vectors come through the packet; head-right is their cross product (as built, M1 C3: `CharacterInstance::face`).
  - The map is mirrored for light from the other side.
- **Hair:** anisotropic specular plus an "angel ring" highlight (a bright band across the hair), with a jitter mask.
- **Eyes:** a parallax / UV-offset refraction fake. Eyes and brows draw over hair through stencil.
- **Rim light:** a depth-difference rim on desktop (it needs the prepass); an N·V rim on mobile.
- **Character light rig:** every character gets a virtual key light whose color and intensity are curved by time of day. Shadow maps bias the ramp threshold rather than multiplying the color.
- **As built (M1 W5, `sapphire_render::ambient`, `shaders/ambient.wgsl`, `probes.wgsl`, `occlusion.wgsl`; look pillars 1 and 4):** the ambient probe grid, the sky cube prefiltered for reflections, capsule AO and GTAO. The module docs give the details; the decisions:
  - **A sparse brick grid.** The cooker keeps only bricks of 4³ cells near geometry (§5.27): the probes stand where surfaces are, on both sides of a thin wall, and the empty sky and solid ground cost nothing. A brick table indirects every brick position, so one 3D atlas holds the kept bricks, each as 5³ texels with its shared faces, so hardware trilinear filtering never crosses into another brick; the same indirection streams bricks later (open worlds). The courtyard's grid (desktop cook, 1.5 m) holds 6,973 probes in 84 of its 128 brick positions; the mobile cook's (3 m) 1,445 in all 16.
  - **SH order per tier.** Sky visibility is L2 in the desktop cook and L1 in the mobile cook (3 m spacing, 128 rays): L2 keeps an arch's or a roof's sky edge where it is, which a 1.5 m grid resolves and a 3 m one doesn't. Bounce is L1 per channel everywhere: it is the low-frequency light the look wants (colored from below, from the side), and three L1s relight in one pass.
  - **Relit as the light changes, in one compute pass.** One compute pass before the main pass (`ambient`, after the sky cube's face and the scene prepass) holds every ambient dispatch of the frame, each only when due: the sky cube projected to L2 and scaled so an open, up-facing surface receives exactly time of day's authored ambient sky in the L1 the grid holds, as from the hemisphere; the reflection prefilter (below); the relight, visibility × that sky (an exact L2 transfer on the icosahedron's vertices) plus the bounce of the frame's keys (`FramePacket::probe_keys`); each character's ambient; and GTAO. Bounce blends keys the world resolved (R4); the grid's sky follows the sky's glows and clouds.
    - **The projection** goes a face at a time: the sky light keeps each cube face's sums, and a frame projects only the faces the sky cube drew (one a frame on desktop, one every other frame on Mobile-Low, all six when a grid arrives), then sums the six. It is one workgroup (since the WARP ambient-cost fix below, three dispatches without workgroup memory: a workgroup a face, then the faces' add-up, then the sky light), so its cost is latency: projecting the whole cube every frame cost about 0.2–0.45 ms of the frame on Desktop-High, by switching it off on alternate frames; a face takes about 50 µs by the pass's own timestamps.
    - **The relight is amortized** (`RelightSchedule`). Every brick relights the frame a grid arrives or the light jumps: the keys' weights, or the authored ambient sky by 5% in a channel from one frame to the next, when the sky is projected again at once, so a time skip relights everything that frame. After a smaller change (a face projected, the keys drifting with the hour) a quarter of the bricks relights a frame in turn, until every quarter has since the last change; while nothing changes, nothing runs. A brick's light is then at most three frames older than its neighbors', while the sky cube itself takes six frames (twelve on Mobile-Low) to follow the sky. The prefilter was already amortized the same way: it filters only the faces the sky cube drew.
    - **Allocations and uploads:** the pass's uniforms (the ambient, relight and GTAO structs and the prefilter's per mip) are one buffer of 256-byte regions staged with one write in the frames they change, and its bind groups are made once: when a grid arrives, and GTAO's per set of pooled prepass views, the last two seen (post's caching). The pass costs about 67 allocations a frame on Desktop-High in the arcade under its grid, 79 with GTAO, and 55 on Mobile-Low, against about 100 for the four passes before (Desktop-High 640.3 a frame then, 573.9 now; Mobile-Low 410.9 and 365.3); GTAO's pass alone 46. What remains is wgpu's recording of the pass: about 4–6 a dispatch (4 the projection's, 6 the relight's), and the rest the pass's own and the prefilter's five mips.
  - **The lookup** (`ambient.wgsl`'s `grid_lookup`) replaces the hemisphere's irradiance in the environment shader where a grid reaches, by the trilinear share of the cell's corners inside the kept bricks: the renderer marks the probes that border an empty brick or the grid's edge at upload (`PROBE_EDGE_BIT`, a bit cooked grids leave clear), so the grid fades into the hemisphere over the last cell before them, and a character rising past the geometry the bake kept bricks around doesn't pop between the two (the grid used to switch off at an empty brick's face). Where the leak test drops every corner, the point takes the hemisphere, not the rejected probes. W4's shading model is otherwise unchanged (ambient terms only). Leaks: the lookup moves off the surface along its normal and toward the camera, then weighs the cell's eight probes by trilinear distance times an axis leak test (a probe whose free distance toward the point is shorter than the way there drops out: a thin wall), DDGI's backface weight and validity, and samples the weights' centroid through one hardware-filtered fetch per sub-volume. Cells the relight flags open (every corner valid, inside the kept bricks and seeing the others) skip the test's reads. Content without a grid keeps the hemisphere: the grid is a pipeline constant, so those pipelines are the shaders as before W5.
  - **Per vertex on Mobile-Low** (`ProbeLookup`, the tiers' `environment.probes`). **Decision, by the numbers:** on Mobile-Low in the arcade at 1080p (mobile cook, release, M5) the per-pixel lookup took the main pass from about 305 µs to 490 µs, 38% of it; read per vertex instead, along the vertex's normal with its irradiance and weight interpolated, the main pass takes 303 µs, the lookup about 10 µs by switching it off on alternate frames. The mobile cook's grid is 3 m between probes, no finer than the world's vertices (the courtyard's ground is 2.3 m squares), so little is lost: the same frame read per pixel differs by at most 23 code values (a mean of 3.7 over half the pixels), inside the arcade where large faces interpolate between their corners. The ambient specular keeps the hemisphere per vertex, which Mobile-Low doesn't draw (its specular is off). The grid's bindings (frame group 11–13 and 16) are vertex-visible for it. Desktop and Mobile-High read per pixel.
  - **Characters** keep C2's stylized hemisphere (flattened toward the camera, clamped in HSV); the grid only decides what it is. The character ambient pass reads the grid at the body's center and the head and writes each character's sky, ground and axis into its `CharacterData` entry (now 224 bytes), which the fragment stage already binds; per-pixel lookups would print the grid's cells on faces. Without a grid the entry holds exactly the frame's hemisphere, so characters shade as before. Characters take capsule AO from the other bodies as one factor, and no GTAO; nor do they occlude the world through GTAO (below).
  - **Reflections:** with a grid and environment specular, the sky cube's faces drawn each frame are prefiltered into a reflection cube's roughness mips (GGX, 32 samples a texel; 128² on the desktop tiers and 64² on Mobile-High; Mobile-Low, without environment specular, makes none; it is made with the first grid, since nothing reads it without one), and the environment's ambient specular reads the mip for its roughness where the grid sees the sky along the reflection, the grid's irradiance where geometry blocks it. C7's reflection add reads it too: a reflective character material in the draw list makes and prefilters it on any tier, grid or not (§5.12's C7 note); reflection probes are early M2.
  - **Ambient occlusion** (`AmbientOcclusion`, per tier): capsule AO on every tier (the nearest 16 bodies as spheres at their axes' nearest points, Quílez's sphere occlusion, gone by five radii) and GTAO at half resolution on the desktop tiers (2 slices × 4 steps, 1 m) and Mobile-High (1 × 4, 0.75 m): a depth prefilter into four mips of view distance, the search, a 5 × 5 depth-aware blur whose weights cancel the 4 × 4 pattern, and a depth-aware upsample; the pattern moves every frame under temporal AA. Both darken the environment's and foliage's ambient alone, the diffuse through Jimenez's multi-bounce fit and the environment's specular through Lagarde's specular occlusion, layered on the grid's sky visibility, which carries occlusion beyond GTAO's radius. `--ao off|capsule|full` is the player's setting (`QualitySettings::ambient_occlusion`). GTAO's samples step a whole half-resolution texel at a time (the nearest at 2 × 1.3 pixels), so each reads the depth of the pixel it is rebuilt at: stepping on full-resolution pixels had read a pixel toward −x and −y for odd offsets, a false horizon of up to about 37° at 1080p that darkened surfaces receding right or down and lightened their mirrors. Transparent environment surfaces, which write no prepass depth, take capsule AO alone, and the upsample takes open sky where no half-resolution texel lies within 10% of the pixel's depth. Alpha-tested surfaces occlude as the prepass draws them, so GTAO relies on it cutting their holes out: foliage's cards are cut out there as the main pass cuts them (W6), so a card occludes with its leaves and never its quad.
